    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F42, R46.4\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, I guess what brings me in is that I keep getting up when I'm really supposed to just stay put. It happens a lot—during client interviews, tutoring sessions, even at my book club. It's getting to the point where people are starting to notice that I can't just sit and listen, and it's becoming a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uneasy when you're supposed to sit and listen in these situations?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel anxious or uneasy, exactly. It's not like I'm sitting there worrying or feeling stressed. I just... I don't know, I just feel like I need to move. It's more of a physical urge to get up rather than an emotional feeling of anxiety. I just can't seem to stay in the chair.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain movements or actions over and over?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. Um, I don't really repeat any specific actions over and over. It's not like a habit or a twitch or anything like that. I just... I just have that strong urge to get out of my seat and move around. But as far as repeating certain movements, no, I haven't noticed doing anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have a lot of extra energy that you need to burn off when you get these urges to get up and move?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess you could say that. It's like I always have to be doing something. Even on days when I don't have field work for an investigation, I can't just relax. I'll go from unpacking boxes to reorganizing all my case files, and then I'm off running errands... it's like I'm still out chasing a lead. I just have a really hard time being off duty and sitting still.\n\nDoctor: Are you getting up to do something specific when you feel the urge to move?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. It's not like I suddenly remember I need to make a phone call or check a file. I just... I just stand up because sitting there is driving me crazy. Sometimes I'll just pace around the room or walk down the hall and back. There's no real purpose or goal to it, other than just needing to get out of the chair.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up to do something in particular when you give in to the urge to move?\n\nPatient: No, like I was just saying, there's really nothing in particular I'm getting up to do. Um, I don't have a specific task in mind when it happens. It's not task-driven at all... I just need to be on my feet because sitting still gets too uncomfortable. It's just the physical need to move, not because I actually need to go do something.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up because you want to do something active?\n\nPatient: No, it's really not about wanting to do an activity or, you know, get a workout in. Like I said, it's just this restless feeling. I don't get up because I want to go for a run or do jumping jacks or something. It's just that I can't stay in the chair anymore.\n\nDoctor: Are these episodes of getting up and moving around causing problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. It's starting to cost me professionally, to be honest. Um, I've actually missed details in my investigations because of it. And it's irritated some of my clients when we're meeting and I just can't stay seated. It's affecting things at the office, too. The junior detectives... they're becoming a lot less willing to debrief with me. I think they just feel steamrolled because I'm up and pacing and just trying to push through the conversation. So, yeah, it's definitely causing some real issues for me at work.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when you're unable to get up and move around in these situations?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to quantify it. Um, it's not like I'm in pain or panicking, but it's just... incredibly uncomfortable. Like an itch you can't scratch, I guess. I wouldn't say I'm in extreme distress, but it's definitely aggravating enough that I just have to move.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty completing tasks or achieving your goals due to your need to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I still get the job done. I close my cases. It's not like I'm abandoning an investigation halfway through just because I had to get up and pace around. Um, like I said, it makes things a bit harder, and it definitely annoys people, but I still finish what I start. So, no, I wouldn't say it's keeping me from completing my tasks or reaching my goals.\n\nDoctor: Are your personal relationships, outside of work, being affected by your inability to sit still?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Um, my friends and family are pretty used to it by now. If we're at a restaurant or watching a movie and I just have to stand up or pace for a minute, they don't really make a big deal out of it. They just know that's how I am. So, I haven't really noticed it causing any friction in my personal life... it's mostly just the professional side of things where it's a problem.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F42, R46.4\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, I guess what brings me in is that I keep getting up when I'm really supposed to just stay put. It happens a lot—during client interviews, tutoring sessions, even at my book club. It's getting to the point where people are starting to notice that I can't just sit and listen, and it's becoming a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uneasy when you're supposed to sit and listen in these situations?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel anxious or uneasy, exactly. It's not like I'm sitting there worrying or feeling stressed. I just... I don't know, I just feel like I need to move. It's more of a physical urge to get up rather than an emotional feeling of anxiety. I just can't seem to stay in the chair.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain movements or actions over and over?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. Um, I don't really repeat any specific actions over and over. It's not like a habit or a twitch or anything like that. I just... I just have that strong urge to get out of my seat and move around. But as far as repeating certain movements, no, I haven't noticed doing anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have a lot of extra energy that you need to burn off when you get these urges to get up and move?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess you could say that. It's like I always have to be doing something. Even on days when I don't have field work for an investigation, I can't just relax. I'll go from unpacking boxes to reorganizing all my case files, and then I'm off running errands... it's like I'm still out chasing a lead. I just have a really hard time being off duty and sitting still.\n\nDoctor: Are you getting up to do something specific when you feel the urge to move?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. It's not like I suddenly remember I need to make a phone call or check a file. I just... I just stand up because sitting there is driving me crazy. Sometimes I'll just pace around the room or walk down the hall and back. There's no real purpose or goal to it, other than just needing to get out of the chair.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up to do something in particular when you give in to the urge to move?\n\nPatient: No, like I was just saying, there's really nothing in particular I'm getting up to do. Um, I don't have a specific task in mind when it happens. It's not task-driven at all... I just need to be on my feet because sitting still gets too uncomfortable. It's just the physical need to move, not because I actually need to go do something.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up because you want to do something active?\n\nPatient: No, it's really not about wanting to do an activity or, you know, get a workout in. Like I said, it's just this restless feeling. I don't get up because I want to go for a run or do jumping jacks or something. It's just that I can't stay in the chair anymore.\n\nDoctor: Are these episodes of getting up and moving around causing problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. So, yeah, it's definitely causing some real issues for me at work.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when you're unable to get up and move around in these situations?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to quantify it. But as far as repeating certain movements, no, I haven't noticed doing anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have a lot of extra energy that you need to burn off when you get these urges to get up and move?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess you could say that. It's like I always have to be doing something. Even on days when I don't have field work for an investigation, I can't just relax. I'll go from unpacking boxes to reorganizing all my case files, and then I'm off running errands... it's like I'm still out chasing a lead. I just have a really hard time being off duty and sitting still.\n\nDoctor: Are you getting up to do something specific when you feel the urge to move?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. It's not like I suddenly remember I need to make a phone call or check a file. I just... I just stand up because sitting there is driving me crazy. Sometimes I'll just pace around the room or walk down the hall and back. There's no real purpose or goal to it, other than just needing to get out of the chair.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up to do something in particular when you give in to the urge to move?\n\nPatient: No, like I was just saying, there's really nothing in particular I'm getting up to do. Um, I don't have a specific task in mind when it happens. It's not task-driven at all... I just need to be on my feet because sitting still gets too uncomfortable. It's just the physical need to move, not because I actually need to go do something.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up because you want to do something active?\n\nPatient: No, it's really not about wanting to do an activity or, you know, get a workout in. Like I said, it's just this restless feeling. I don't get up because I want to go for a run or do jumping jacks or something. It's just that I can't stay in the chair anymore.\n\nDoctor: Are these episodes of getting up and moving around causing problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. It's starting to cost me professionally, to be honest. Um, I've actually missed details in my investigations because of it. And it's irritated some of my clients when we're meeting and I just can't stay seated. It's affecting things at the office, too. The junior detectives... they're becoming a lot less willing to debrief with me. I think they just feel steamrolled because I'm up and pacing and just trying to push through the conversation. So, yeah, it's definitely causing some real issues for me at work.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when you're unable to get up and move around in these situations?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to quantify it. Um, it's not like I'm in pain or panicking, but it's just... incredibly uncomfortable. Like an itch you can't scratch, I guess. I wouldn't say I'm in extreme distress, but it's definitely aggravating enough that I just have to move.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty completing tasks or achieving your goals due to your need to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I still get the job done. I close my cases. It's not like I'm abandoning an investigation halfway through just because I had to get up and pace around. Um, like I said, it makes things a bit harder, and it definitely annoys people, but I still finish what I start. So, no, I wouldn't say it's keeping me from completing my tasks or reaching my goals.\n\nDoctor: Are your personal relationships, outside of work, being affected by your inability to sit still?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Um, my friends and family are pretty used to it by now. If we're at a restaurant or watching a movie and I just have to stand up or pace for a minute, they don't really make a big deal out of it. They just know that's how I am. So, I haven't really noticed it causing any friction in my personal life... it's mostly just the professional side of things where it's a problem.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this strong urge to get up and move around in situations where you're supposed to sit still?\n\nPatient: It's been noticeably worse for about eight months now. Um, it really started picking up right around the time I had to move out of my old apartment in Chicago. Before that, I mean, I've always been a little restless, but since that move, it's just been a lot harder to stay put when I need to.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still or exhibited excessive fidgeting as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely did. Um, my old grade-school report cards always said pretty much the same thing—that I was bright, but I just couldn't stay in my seat or wait my turn. My mother actually still jokes about it. She says I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through. So, yeah, it was definitely an issue when I was a kid, too.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

It happens a lot—during client interviews, tutoring sessions, even at my book club. It's getting to the point where people are starting to notice that I can't just sit and listen, and it's becoming a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uneasy when you're supposed to sit and listen in these situations?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel anxious or uneasy, exactly. It's not like I'm sitting there worrying or feeling stressed. I just... I don't know, I just feel like I need to move. It's more of a physical urge to get up rather than an emotional feeling of anxiety. It's like I always have to be doing something. Even on days when I don't have field work for an investigation, I can't just relax. I'll go from unpacking boxes to reorganizing all my case files, and then I'm off running errands... it's like I'm still out chasing a lead. I just have a really hard time being off duty and sitting still.\n\nDoctor: Are you getting up to do something specific when you feel the urge to move?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. It's not like I suddenly remember I need to make a phone call or check a file. I just... I just stand up because sitting there is driving me crazy. Sometimes I'll just pace around the room or walk down the hall and back. There's no real purpose or goal to it, other than just needing to get out of the chair.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up to do something in particular when you give in to the urge to move?\n\nPatient: No, like I was just saying, there's really nothing in particular I'm getting up to do. Um, I don't have a specific task in mind when it happens. It's not task-driven at all... I just need to be on my feet because sitting still gets too uncomfortable. It's just the physical need to move, not because I actually need to go do something.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up because you want to do something active?\n\nPatient: No, it's really not about wanting to do an activity or, you know, get a workout in. Like I said, it's just this restless feeling. I don't get up because I want to go for a run or do jumping jacks or something. It's just that I can't stay in the chair anymore.\n\nDoctor: Are these episodes of getting up and moving around causing problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. It's starting to cost me professionally, to be honest. Um, I've actually missed details in my investigations because of it. Um, it's not like I'm in pain or panicking, but it's just... incredibly uncomfortable. Like an itch you can't scratch, I guess. I wouldn't say I'm in extreme distress, but it's definitely aggravating enough that I just have to move.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty completing tasks or achieving your goals due to your need to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I still get the job done. I close my cases. It's not like I'm abandoning an investigation halfway through just because I had to get up and pace around. Um, like I said, it makes things a bit harder, and it definitely annoys people, but I still finish what I start. So, no, I wouldn't say it's keeping me from completing my tasks or reaching my goals.\n\nDoctor: Are your personal relationships, outside of work, being affected by your inability to sit still?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Um, my friends and family are pretty used to it by now. If we're at a restaurant or watching a movie and I just have to stand up or pace for a minute, they don't really make a big deal out of it. They just know that's how I am. So, I haven't really noticed it causing any friction in my personal life... it's mostly just the professional side of things where it's a problem.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this strong urge to get up and move around in situations where you're supposed to sit still?\n\nPatient: It's been noticeably worse for about eight months now. Um, it really started picking up right around the time I had to move out of my old apartment in Chicago. Before that, I mean, I've always been a little restless, but since that move, it's just been a lot harder to stay put when I need to.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still or exhibited excessive fidgeting as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely did. Um, my old grade-school report cards always said pretty much the same thing—that I was bright, but I just couldn't stay in my seat or wait my turn. My mother actually still jokes about it. She says I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through. So, yeah, it was definitely an issue when I was a kid, too.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you when you're supposed to be focused on something?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Um, I don't usually have an issue with getting distracted. With my line of work—I'm a private investigator—I actually have to stay pretty focused on the details of whatever I'm looking at, and I can do that just fine. It's really just the physical need to move around that gets to me, not my mind wandering off or losing focus.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It's just the physical need to move, not because I actually need to go do something.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up because you want to do something active?\n\nPatient: No, it's really not about wanting to do an activity or, you know, get a workout in. Like I said, it's just this restless feeling. I don't get up because I want to go for a run or do jumping jacks or something. It's just that I can't stay in the chair anymore.\n\nDoctor: Are these episodes of getting up and moving around causing problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. It's starting to cost me professionally, to be honest. Um, I've actually missed details in my investigations because of it. And it's irritated some of my clients when we're meeting and I just can't stay seated. It's affecting things at the office, too. The junior detectives... they're becoming a lot less willing to debrief with me. I think they just feel steamrolled because I'm up and pacing and just trying to push through the conversation. So, yeah, it's definitely causing some real issues for me at work.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when you're unable to get up and move around in these situations?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to quantify it. Um, it's not like I'm in pain or panicking, but it's just... incredibly uncomfortable. Like an itch you can't scratch, I guess. I wouldn't say I'm in extreme distress, but it's definitely aggravating enough that I just have to move.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty completing tasks or achieving your goals due to your need to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I still get the job done. I close my cases. It's not like I'm abandoning an investigation halfway through just because I had to get up and pace around. Um, like I said, it makes things a bit harder, and it definitely annoys people, but I still finish what I start. So, no, I wouldn't say it's keeping me from completing my tasks or reaching my goals.\n\nDoctor: Are your personal relationships, outside of work, being affected by your inability to sit still?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Um, my friends and family are pretty used to it by now. If we're at a restaurant or watching a movie and I just have to stand up or pace for a minute, they don't really make a big deal out of it. They just know that's how I am. So, I haven't really noticed it causing any friction in my personal life... it's mostly just the professional side of things where it's a problem.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this strong urge to get up and move around in situations where you're supposed to sit still?\n\nPatient: It's been noticeably worse for about eight months now. Um, it really started picking up right around the time I had to move out of my old apartment in Chicago. Before that, I mean, I've always been a little restless, but since that move, it's just been a lot harder to stay put when I need to.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still or exhibited excessive fidgeting as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely did. Um, my old grade-school report cards always said pretty much the same thing—that I was bright, but I just couldn't stay in my seat or wait my turn. My mother actually still jokes about it. She says I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through. So, yeah, it was definitely an issue when I was a kid, too.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you when you're supposed to be focused on something?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Um, I don't usually have an issue with getting distracted. With my line of work—I'm a private investigator—I actually have to stay pretty focused on the details of whatever I'm looking at, and I can do that just fine. It's really just the physical need to move around that gets to me, not my mind wandering off or losing focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty sustaining attention on tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: No, I don't. Um, it's pretty much like what I was just saying—my attention span is actually fine. I can stick with a task or an investigation for hours without a problem. It's really just that I feel like I need to be pacing or moving around while I'm doing it. But sustaining the actual focus isn't an issue for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

She says I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through. So, yeah, it was definitely an issue when I was a kid, too.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you when you're supposed to be focused on something?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Um, I don't usually have an issue with getting distracted. With my line of work—I'm a private investigator—I actually have to stay pretty focused on the details of whatever I'm looking at, and I can do that just fine. It's really just the physical need to move around that gets to me, not my mind wandering off or losing focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty sustaining attention on tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: No, I don't. Um, it's pretty much like what I was just saying—my attention span is actually fine. I can stick with a task or an investigation for hours without a problem. It's really just that I feel like I need to be pacing or moving around while I'm doing it. But sustaining the actual focus isn't an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say so. Um, I might be physically restless, but I don't just jump into things blindly. In my job, you really can't afford to make impulsive decisions—you have to be pretty methodical and think about the fallout before you make a move. So, no, I don't really have a problem with acting without thinking.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.     "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F90.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, I guess what brings me in is that I keep getting up when I'm really supposed to just stay put. It happens a lot—during client interviews, tutoring sessions, even at my book club. It's getting to the point where people are starting to notice that I can't just sit and listen, and it's becoming a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uneasy when you're supposed to sit and listen in these situations?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel anxious or uneasy, exactly. It's not like I'm sitting there worrying or feeling stressed. I just... I don't know, I just feel like I need to move. It's more of a physical urge to get up rather than an emotional feeling of anxiety. I just can't seem to stay in the chair.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain movements or actions over and over?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. Um, I don't really repeat any specific actions over and over. It's not like a habit or a twitch or anything like that. I just... I just have that strong urge to get out of my seat and move around. But as far as repeating certain movements, no, I haven't noticed doing anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have a lot of extra energy that you need to burn off when you get these urges to get up and move?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess you could say that. It's like I always have to be doing something. Even on days when I don't have field work for an investigation, I can't just relax. I'll go from unpacking boxes to reorganizing all my case files, and then I'm off running errands... it's like I'm still out chasing a lead. I just have a really hard time being off duty and sitting still.\n\nDoctor: Are you getting up to do something specific when you feel the urge to move?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. It's not like I suddenly remember I need to make a phone call or check a file. I just... I just stand up because sitting there is driving me crazy. Sometimes I'll just pace around the room or walk down the hall and back. There's no real purpose or goal to it, other than just needing to get out of the chair.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up to do something in particular when you give in to the urge to move?\n\nPatient: No, like I was just saying, there's really nothing in particular I'm getting up to do. Um, I don't have a specific task in mind when it happens. It's not task-driven at all... I just need to be on my feet because sitting still gets too uncomfortable. It's just the physical need to move, not because I actually need to go do something.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up because you want to do something active?\n\nPatient: No, it's really not about wanting to do an activity or, you know, get a workout in. Like I said, it's just this restless feeling. I don't get up because I want to go for a run or do jumping jacks or something. It's just that I can't stay in the chair anymore.\n\nDoctor: Are these episodes of getting up and moving around causing problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. It's starting to cost me professionally, to be honest. Um, I've actually missed details in my investigations because of it. And it's irritated some of my clients when we're meeting and I just can't stay seated. It's affecting things at the office, too. The junior detectives... they're becoming a lot less willing to debrief with me. I think they just feel steamrolled because I'm up and pacing and just trying to push through the conversation. So, yeah, it's definitely causing some real issues for me at work.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when you're unable to get up and move around in these situations?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to quantify it. Um, it's not like I'm in pain or panicking, but it's just... incredibly uncomfortable. Like an itch you can't scratch, I guess. I wouldn't say I'm in extreme distress, but it's definitely aggravating enough that I just have to move.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty completing tasks or achieving your goals due to your need to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I still get the job done. I close my cases. It's not like I'm abandoning an investigation halfway through just because I had to get up and pace around. Um, like I said, it makes things a bit harder, and it definitely annoys people, but I still finish what I start. So, no, I wouldn't say it's keeping me from completing my tasks or reaching my goals.\n\nDoctor: Are your personal relationships, outside of work, being affected by your inability to sit still?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Um, my friends and family are pretty used to it by now. If we're at a restaurant or watching a movie and I just have to stand up or pace for a minute, they don't really make a big deal out of it. They just know that's how I am. So, I haven't really noticed it causing any friction in my personal life... it's mostly just the professional side of things where it's a problem.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this strong urge to get up and move around in situations where you're supposed to sit still?\n\nPatient: It's been noticeably worse for about eight months now. Um, it really started picking up right around the time I had to move out of my old apartment in Chicago. Before that, I mean, I've always been a little restless, but since that move, it's just been a lot harder to stay put when I need to.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still or exhibited excessive fidgeting as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely did. Um, my old grade-school report cards always said pretty much the same thing—that I was bright, but I just couldn't stay in my seat or wait my turn. My mother actually still jokes about it. She says I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through. So, yeah, it was definitely an issue when I was a kid, too.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you when you're supposed to be focused on something?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Um, I don't usually have an issue with getting distracted. With my line of work—I'm a private investigator—I actually have to stay pretty focused on the details of whatever I'm looking at, and I can do that just fine. It's really just the physical need to move around that gets to me, not my mind wandering off or losing focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty sustaining attention on tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: No, I don't. So, no, I don't really have a problem with acting without thinking.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to engage in quiet leisure activities, such as reading or listening to music, because you feel the need to be active?\n\nPatient: Yes, that's exactly it. I just have a really hard time sitting quietly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F90.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, I guess what brings me in is that I keep getting up when I'm really supposed to just stay put. It happens a lot—during client interviews, tutoring sessions, even at my book club. It's getting to the point where people are starting to notice that I can't just sit and listen, and it's becoming a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uneasy when you're supposed to sit and listen in these situations?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel anxious or uneasy, exactly. It's not like I'm sitting there worrying or feeling stressed. I just... I don't know, I just feel like I need to move. It's more of a physical urge to get up rather than an emotional feeling of anxiety. I just can't seem to stay in the chair.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain movements or actions over and over?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. Um, I don't really repeat any specific actions over and over. It's not like a habit or a twitch or anything like that. I just... I just have that strong urge to get out of my seat and move around. But as far as repeating certain movements, no, I haven't noticed doing anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have a lot of extra energy that you need to burn off when you get these urges to get up and move?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess you could say that. It's like I always have to be doing something. Even on days when I don't have field work for an investigation, I can't just relax. I'll go from unpacking boxes to reorganizing all my case files, and then I'm off running errands... it's like I'm still out chasing a lead. I just have a really hard time being off duty and sitting still.\n\nDoctor: Are you getting up to do something specific when you feel the urge to move?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. It's not like I suddenly remember I need to make a phone call or check a file. I just... I just stand up because sitting there is driving me crazy. Sometimes I'll just pace around the room or walk down the hall and back. There's no real purpose or goal to it, other than just needing to get out of the chair.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up to do something in particular when you give in to the urge to move?\n\nPatient: No, like I was just saying, there's really nothing in particular I'm getting up to do. Um, I don't have a specific task in mind when it happens. It's not task-driven at all... I just need to be on my feet because sitting still gets too uncomfortable. It's just the physical need to move, not because I actually need to go do something.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up because you want to do something active?\n\nPatient: No, it's really not about wanting to do an activity or, you know, get a workout in. Like I said, it's just this restless feeling. I don't get up because I want to go for a run or do jumping jacks or something. It's just that I can't stay in the chair anymore.\n\nDoctor: Are these episodes of getting up and moving around causing problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. It's starting to cost me professionally, to be honest. Um, I've actually missed details in my investigations because of it. And it's irritated some of my clients when we're meeting and I just can't stay seated. It's affecting things at the office, too. The junior detectives... they're becoming a lot less willing to debrief with me. I think they just feel steamrolled because I'm up and pacing and just trying to push through the conversation. So, yeah, it's definitely causing some real issues for me at work.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when you're unable to get up and move around in these situations?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to quantify it. Um, it's not like I'm in pain or panicking, but it's just... incredibly uncomfortable. Like an itch you can't scratch, I guess. I wouldn't say I'm in extreme distress, but it's definitely aggravating enough that I just have to move.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty completing tasks or achieving your goals due to your need to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I still get the job done. I close my cases. It's not like I'm abandoning an investigation halfway through just because I had to get up and pace around. Um, like I said, it makes things a bit harder, and it definitely annoys people, but I still finish what I start. So, no, I wouldn't say it's keeping me from completing my tasks or reaching my goals.\n\nDoctor: Are your personal relationships, outside of work, being affected by your inability to sit still?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Um, my friends and family are pretty used to it by now. If we're at a restaurant or watching a movie and I just have to stand up or pace for a minute, they don't really make a big deal out of it. They just know that's how I am. So, I haven't really noticed it causing any friction in my personal life... it's mostly just the professional side of things where it's a problem.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this strong urge to get up and move around in situations where you're supposed to sit still?\n\nPatient: It's been noticeably worse for about eight months now. Um, it really started picking up right around the time I had to move out of my old apartment in Chicago. Before that, I mean, I've always been a little restless, but since that move, it's just been a lot harder to stay put when I need to.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still or exhibited excessive fidgeting as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely did. Um, my old grade-school report cards always said pretty much the same thing—that I was bright, but I just couldn't stay in my seat or wait my turn. My mother actually still jokes about it. She says I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through. So, yeah, it was definitely an issue when I was a kid, too.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you when you're supposed to be focused on something?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Um, I don't usually have an issue with getting distracted. With my line of work—I'm a private investigator—I actually have to stay pretty focused on the details of whatever I'm looking at, and I can do that just fine. It's really just the physical need to move around that gets to me, not my mind wandering off or losing focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty sustaining attention on tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: No, I don't. Um, it's pretty much like what I was just saying—my attention span is actually fine. I can stick with a task or an investigation for hours without a problem. It's really just that I feel like I need to be pacing or moving around while I'm doing it. But sustaining the actual focus isn't an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say so. So, no, I don't really have a problem with acting without thinking.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to engage in quiet leisure activities, such as reading or listening to music, because you feel the need to be active?\n\nPatient: Yes, that's exactly it. Um, quiet things that used to help me unwind—like yoga or going to my book club—they've become pretty difficult lately. Instead of just being still and enjoying it, it usually turns into me whispering comments to whoever is next to me, rustling my papers, or just finding some excuse to get up and move around. I just have a really hard time sitting quietly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're constantly 'on the go' even when you're not necessarily getting up and moving around in situations?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I'll move straight from unpacking boxes, to reorganizing my case files, to running errands, almost like I'm actively chasing a lead. I just have a really hard time being off duty.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F90.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, I guess what brings me in is that I keep getting up when I'm really supposed to just stay put. It happens a lot—during client interviews, tutoring sessions, even at my book club. It's getting to the point where people are starting to notice that I can't just sit and listen, and it's becoming a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uneasy when you're supposed to sit and listen in these situations?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel anxious or uneasy, exactly. It's not like I'm sitting there worrying or feeling stressed. I just... I don't know, I just feel like I need to move. It's more of a physical urge to get up rather than an emotional feeling of anxiety. I just can't seem to stay in the chair.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain movements or actions over and over?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. Um, I don't really repeat any specific actions over and over. It's not like a habit or a twitch or anything like that. I just... I just have that strong urge to get out of my seat and move around. But as far as repeating certain movements, no, I haven't noticed doing anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have a lot of extra energy that you need to burn off when you get these urges to get up and move?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess you could say that. It's like I always have to be doing something. Even on days when I don't have field work for an investigation, I can't just relax. I'll go from unpacking boxes to reorganizing all my case files, and then I'm off running errands... it's like I'm still out chasing a lead. I just have a really hard time being off duty and sitting still.\n\nDoctor: Are you getting up to do something specific when you feel the urge to move?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. It's not like I suddenly remember I need to make a phone call or check a file. I just... I just stand up because sitting there is driving me crazy. Sometimes I'll just pace around the room or walk down the hall and back. There's no real purpose or goal to it, other than just needing to get out of the chair.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up to do something in particular when you give in to the urge to move?\n\nPatient: No, like I was just saying, there's really nothing in particular I'm getting up to do. Um, I don't have a specific task in mind when it happens. It's not task-driven at all... I just need to be on my feet because sitting still gets too uncomfortable. It's just the physical need to move, not because I actually need to go do something.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up because you want to do something active?\n\nPatient: No, it's really not about wanting to do an activity or, you know, get a workout in. Like I said, it's just this restless feeling. I don't get up because I want to go for a run or do jumping jacks or something. It's just that I can't stay in the chair anymore.\n\nDoctor: Are these episodes of getting up and moving around causing problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. It's starting to cost me professionally, to be honest. Um, I've actually missed details in my investigations because of it. And it's irritated some of my clients when we're meeting and I just can't stay seated. It's affecting things at the office, too. The junior detectives... they're becoming a lot less willing to debrief with me. I think they just feel steamrolled because I'm up and pacing and just trying to push through the conversation. So, yeah, it's definitely causing some real issues for me at work.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when you're unable to get up and move around in these situations?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to quantify it. Um, it's not like I'm in pain or panicking, but it's just... incredibly uncomfortable. Like an itch you can't scratch, I guess. I wouldn't say I'm in extreme distress, but it's definitely aggravating enough that I just have to move.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty completing tasks or achieving your goals due to your need to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I still get the job done. I close my cases. It's not like I'm abandoning an investigation halfway through just because I had to get up and pace around. Um, like I said, it makes things a bit harder, and it definitely annoys people, but I still finish what I start. So, no, I wouldn't say it's keeping me from completing my tasks or reaching my goals.\n\nDoctor: Are your personal relationships, outside of work, being affected by your inability to sit still?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Um, my friends and family are pretty used to it by now. If we're at a restaurant or watching a movie and I just have to stand up or pace for a minute, they don't really make a big deal out of it. They just know that's how I am. So, I haven't really noticed it causing any friction in my personal life... it's mostly just the professional side of things where it's a problem.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this strong urge to get up and move around in situations where you're supposed to sit still?\n\nPatient: It's been noticeably worse for about eight months now. Um, it really started picking up right around the time I had to move out of my old apartment in Chicago. Before that, I mean, I've always been a little restless, but since that move, it's just been a lot harder to stay put when I need to.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still or exhibited excessive fidgeting as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely did. Um, my old grade-school report cards always said pretty much the same thing—that I was bright, but I just couldn't stay in my seat or wait my turn. My mother actually still jokes about it. She says I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through. So, yeah, it was definitely an issue when I was a kid, too.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you when you're supposed to be focused on something?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Um, I don't usually have an issue with getting distracted. With my line of work—I'm a private investigator—I actually have to stay pretty focused on the details of whatever I'm looking at, and I can do that just fine. It's really just the physical need to move around that gets to me, not my mind wandering off or losing focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty sustaining attention on tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: No, I don't. Um, it's pretty much like what I was just saying—my attention span is actually fine. I can stick with a task or an investigation for hours without a problem. It's really just that I feel like I need to be pacing or moving around while I'm doing it. But sustaining the actual focus isn't an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say so. Um, I might be physically restless, but I don't just jump into things blindly. In my job, you really can't afford to make impulsive decisions—you have to be pretty methodical and think about the fallout before you make a move. So, no, I don't really have a problem with acting without thinking.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to engage in quiet leisure activities, such as reading or listening to music, because you feel the need to be active?\n\nPatient: Yes, that's exactly it. Um, quiet things that used to help me unwind—like yoga or going to my book club—they've become pretty difficult lately. Instead of just being still and enjoying it, it usually turns into me whispering comments to whoever is next to me, rustling my papers, or just finding some excuse to get up and move around. I just have a really hard time sitting quietly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're constantly 'on the go' even when you're not necessarily getting up and moving around in situations?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Um, even on days when I don't have any actual field work, I'm still just... going. I'll move straight from unpacking boxes, to reorganizing my case files, to running errands, almost like I'm actively chasing a lead. I just have a really hard time being off duty.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and need to move are more about an inner feeling of restlessness or an actual physical need to be active?\n\nPatient: Um, I'm not really sure how to answer that. I haven't really thought about it in those terms. I guess I don't really know the difference between the two. It's just... I just know I need to be doing something. Whether that's coming from my brain or my body, I couldn't really tell you. It all just kind of blends together for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F90.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, I guess what brings me in is that I keep getting up when I'm really supposed to just stay put. It happens a lot—during client interviews, tutoring sessions, even at my book club. It's getting to the point where people are starting to notice that I can't just sit and listen, and it's becoming a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uneasy when you're supposed to sit and listen in these situations?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel anxious or uneasy, exactly. It's not like I'm sitting there worrying or feeling stressed. I just... I don't know, I just feel like I need to move. It's more of a physical urge to get up rather than an emotional feeling of anxiety. I just can't seem to stay in the chair.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain movements or actions over and over?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. Um, I don't really repeat any specific actions over and over. It's not like a habit or a twitch or anything like that. I just... I just have that strong urge to get out of my seat and move around. But as far as repeating certain movements, no, I haven't noticed doing anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have a lot of extra energy that you need to burn off when you get these urges to get up and move?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess you could say that. It's like I always have to be doing something. Even on days when I don't have field work for an investigation, I can't just relax. I'll go from unpacking boxes to reorganizing all my case files, and then I'm off running errands... it's like I'm still out chasing a lead. I just have a really hard time being off duty and sitting still.\n\nDoctor: Are you getting up to do something specific when you feel the urge to move?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. It's not like I suddenly remember I need to make a phone call or check a file. I just... I just stand up because sitting there is driving me crazy. Sometimes I'll just pace around the room or walk down the hall and back. There's no real purpose or goal to it, other than just needing to get out of the chair.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up to do something in particular when you give in to the urge to move?\n\nPatient: No, like I was just saying, there's really nothing in particular I'm getting up to do. Um, I don't have a specific task in mind when it happens. It's not task-driven at all... I just need to be on my feet because sitting still gets too uncomfortable. It's just the physical need to move, not because I actually need to go do something.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up because you want to do something active?\n\nPatient: No, it's really not about wanting to do an activity or, you know, get a workout in. Like I said, it's just this restless feeling. I don't get up because I want to go for a run or do jumping jacks or something. It's just that I can't stay in the chair anymore.\n\nDoctor: Are these episodes of getting up and moving around causing problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. It's starting to cost me professionally, to be honest. Um, I've actually missed details in my investigations because of it. And it's irritated some of my clients when we're meeting and I just can't stay seated. It's affecting things at the office, too. The junior detectives... they're becoming a lot less willing to debrief with me. I think they just feel steamrolled because I'm up and pacing and just trying to push through the conversation. So, yeah, it's definitely causing some real issues for me at work.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when you're unable to get up and move around in these situations?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to quantify it. Um, it's not like I'm in pain or panicking, but it's just... incredibly uncomfortable. Like an itch you can't scratch, I guess. I wouldn't say I'm in extreme distress, but it's definitely aggravating enough that I just have to move.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty completing tasks or achieving your goals due to your need to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I still get the job done. I close my cases. It's not like I'm abandoning an investigation halfway through just because I had to get up and pace around. Um, like I said, it makes things a bit harder, and it definitely annoys people, but I still finish what I start. So, no, I wouldn't say it's keeping me from completing my tasks or reaching my goals.\n\nDoctor: Are your personal relationships, outside of work, being affected by your inability to sit still?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Um, my friends and family are pretty used to it by now. If we're at a restaurant or watching a movie and I just have to stand up or pace for a minute, they don't really make a big deal out of it. They just know that's how I am. So, I haven't really noticed it causing any friction in my personal life... it's mostly just the professional side of things where it's a problem.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this strong urge to get up and move around in situations where you're supposed to sit still?\n\nPatient: It's been noticeably worse for about eight months now. Um, it really started picking up right around the time I had to move out of my old apartment in Chicago. Before that, I mean, I've always been a little restless, but since that move, it's just been a lot harder to stay put when I need to.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still or exhibited excessive fidgeting as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely did. Um, my old grade-school report cards always said pretty much the same thing—that I was bright, but I just couldn't stay in my seat or wait my turn. My mother actually still jokes about it. She says I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through. So, yeah, it was definitely an issue when I was a kid, too.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you when you're supposed to be focused on something?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Um, I don't usually have an issue with getting distracted. With my line of work—I'm a private investigator—I actually have to stay pretty focused on the details of whatever I'm looking at, and I can do that just fine. It's really just the physical need to move around that gets to me, not my mind wandering off or losing focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty sustaining attention on tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: No, I don't. Um, it's pretty much like what I was just saying—my attention span is actually fine. I can stick with a task or an investigation for hours without a problem. It's really just that I feel like I need to be pacing or moving around while I'm doing it. But sustaining the actual focus isn't an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say so. Um, I might be physically restless, but I don't just jump into things blindly. In my job, you really can't afford to make impulsive decisions—you have to be pretty methodical and think about the fallout before you make a move. So, no, I don't really have a problem with acting without thinking.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to engage in quiet leisure activities, such as reading or listening to music, because you feel the need to be active?\n\nPatient: Yes, that's exactly it. Um, quiet things that used to help me unwind—like yoga or going to my book club—they've become pretty difficult lately. Instead of just being still and enjoying it, it usually turns into me whispering comments to whoever is next to me, rustling my papers, or just finding some excuse to get up and move around. I just have a really hard time sitting quietly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're constantly 'on the go' even when you're not necessarily getting up and moving around in situations?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Um, even on days when I don't have any actual field work, I'm still just... going. I'll move straight from unpacking boxes, to reorganizing my case files, to running errands, almost like I'm actively chasing a lead. I just have a really hard time being off duty.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and need to move are more about an inner feeling of restlessness or an actual physical need to be active?\n\nPatient: Um, I'm not really sure how to answer that. I haven't really thought about it in those terms. I guess I don't really know the difference between the two. It's just... I just know I need to be doing something. Whether that's coming from my brain or my body, I couldn't really tell you. It all just kind of blends together for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty following through on instructions or finishing tasks because you lose focus or get distracted?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really have that problem. Um, I'm actually pretty good at following through on things. In my line of work, you kind of have to be. I don't really lose focus or forget what I'm doing... I might be pacing around or tapping my foot while I work on a case, but I always finish the job. So, no, getting distracted like that isn't really an issue for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

So, no, I don't really have a problem with acting without thinking.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to engage in quiet leisure activities, such as reading or listening to music, because you feel the need to be active?\n\nPatient: Yes, that's exactly it. Um, quiet things that used to help me unwind—like yoga or going to my book club—they've become pretty difficult lately. Instead of just being still and enjoying it, it usually turns into me whispering comments to whoever is next to me, rustling my papers, or just finding some excuse to get up and move around. I just have a really hard time sitting quietly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're constantly 'on the go' even when you're not necessarily getting up and moving around in situations?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Um, even on days when I don't have any actual field work, I'm still just... going. I'll move straight from unpacking boxes, to reorganizing my case files, to running errands, almost like I'm actively chasing a lead. I just have a really hard time being off duty.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and need to move are more about an inner feeling of restlessness or an actual physical need to be active?\n\nPatient: Um, I'm not really sure how to answer that. I haven't really thought about it in those terms. I guess I don't really know the difference between the two. It's just... I just know I need to be doing something. Whether that's coming from my brain or my body, I couldn't really tell you. It all just kind of blends together for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty following through on instructions or finishing tasks because you lose focus or get distracted?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really have that problem. Um, I'm actually pretty good at following through on things. In my line of work, you kind of have to be. I don't really lose focus or forget what I'm doing... I might be pacing around or tapping my foot while I work on a case, but I always finish the job. So, no, getting distracted like that isn't really an issue for me."
